Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed part of it and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with keeping up when people talk to you happen all the time or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every second, no. But it happens enough that I notice it. Like at the museum, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble hearing people when there's background noise?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it as a hearing problem exactly… it’s more just that sometimes I miss what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're having trouble paying attention to what people are saying, or do you think you're actually not hearing the words?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more paying attention, maybe. They’re talking right to me, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: When you miss what someone is saying, are you usually thinking about something else at the same time?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess. I’m not always aware of what I’m thinking about. It’s more like they’re talking right to me, and I’m looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: When you miss what someone is saying, is it because something else caught your attention?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, maybe. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them… then I realize I missed part of it. I don’t always know what caught me, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind often wanders to other things when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does. Like, they’ll be talking to me at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about what the other person is talking about when you miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I don’t care. It’s more… they’re talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I still miss the middle somehow. Then I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following conversations if people spoke more slowly?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’m not sure it’s just the speed, though. Even when someone’s talking right to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them and then realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble understanding what people are saying when you're speaking in your native language versus when someone is speaking in a different language or using complex vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think it’s really about the language or big words. I mean, if it’s too technical, sure, anyone might get lost, but that’s not usually what I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting bored when people talk to you, which might cause you to miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s boredom, really. Like at the museum, someone can be telling me about the next art walk and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like at the museum, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble hearing people when there's background noise?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it as a hearing problem exactly… it’s more just that sometimes I miss what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're having trouble paying attention to what people are saying, or do you think you're actually not hearing the words?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more paying attention, maybe. They’re talking right to me, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: When you miss what someone is saying, are you usually thinking about something else at the same time?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess. I’m not always aware of what I’m thinking about. It’s more like they’re talking right to me, and I’m looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: When you miss what someone is saying, is it because something else caught your attention?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, maybe. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them… then I realize I missed part of it. I don’t always know what caught me, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind often wanders to other things when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does. Like, they’ll be talking to me at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about what the other person is talking about when you miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I don’t care. It’s more… they’re talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I still miss the middle somehow. Then I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following conversations if people spoke more slowly?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’m not sure it’s just the speed, though. Even when someone’s talking right to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them and then realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble understanding what people are saying when you're speaking in your native language versus when someone is speaking in a different language or using complex vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think it’s really about the language or big words. I mean, if it’s too technical, sure, anyone might get lost, but that’s not usually what I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting bored when people talk to you, which might cause you to miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s boredom, really. Like at the museum, someone can be telling me about the next art walk and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're interested in what people are talking about when you're having trouble following the conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I am interested. Like with the museum stuff, the next art walk and all that, I do want to hear it. I’ll be looking right at them, and then I realize I missed the middle somehow, so I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your brain is having trouble processing the information being said, or is it more that you're just not focusing on the conversation?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels more like I’m not staying with it, even though I’m trying to. Someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow the conversation better if you could see the words written down or had a chance to repeat them back to yourself?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I’m not really sure. I just know when they’re talking to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them, and then I’ve missed part of it. If I could see it written down, I guess I might not have to ask them to repeat it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I haven’t noticed it as a hearing problem exactly… it’s more just that sometimes I miss what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're having trouble paying attention to what people are saying, or do you think you're actually not hearing the words?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more paying attention, maybe. They’re talking right to me, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: When you miss what someone is saying, are you usually thinking about something else at the same time?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess. I’m not always aware of what I’m thinking about. It’s more like they’re talking right to me, and I’m looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: When you miss what someone is saying, is it because something else caught your attention?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, maybe. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them… then I realize I missed part of it. I don’t always know what caught me, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind often wanders to other things when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does. Like, they’ll be talking to me at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about what the other person is talking about when you miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I don’t care. It’s more… they’re talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I still miss the middle somehow. Then I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following conversations if people spoke more slowly?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’m not sure it’s just the speed, though. Even when someone’s talking right to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them and then realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble understanding what people are saying when you're speaking in your native language versus when someone is speaking in a different language or using complex vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think it’s really about the language or big words. I mean, if it’s too technical, sure, anyone might get lost, but that’s not usually what I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting bored when people talk to you, which might cause you to miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s boredom, really. Like at the museum, someone can be telling me about the next art walk and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're interested in what people are talking about when you're having trouble following the conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I am interested. Like with the museum stuff, the next art walk and all that, I do want to hear it. I’ll be looking right at them, and then I realize I missed the middle somehow, so I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your brain is having trouble processing the information being said, or is it more that you're just not focusing on the conversation?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels more like I’m not staying with it, even though I’m trying to. Someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow the conversation better if you could see the words written down or had a chance to repeat them back to yourself?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I’m not really sure. I just know when they’re talking to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them, and then I’ve missed part of it. If I could see it written down, I guess I might not have to ask them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations in all languages you speak, or is it mostly in one language?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it in those conversations at the museum. Like when someone’s telling me about the next art walk, I’m looking right at them, and then I realize I missed part of it and have to ask them to go over it again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF88, R41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… mostly that I’ve been having trouble keeping up when people talk to me. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed part of it and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Does this trouble with keeping up when people talk to you happen all the time or just sometimes?\n\nPatient: It’s… not every second, no. But it happens enough that I notice it. Like at the museum, someone can be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble hearing people when there's background noise?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed it as a hearing problem exactly… it’s more just that sometimes I miss what was said.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're having trouble paying attention to what people are saying, or do you think you're actually not hearing the words?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more paying attention, maybe. They’re talking right to me, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: When you miss what someone is saying, are you usually thinking about something else at the same time?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, I guess. I’m not always aware of what I’m thinking about. It’s more like they’re talking right to me, and I’m looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: When you miss what someone is saying, is it because something else caught your attention?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, maybe. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them… then I realize I missed part of it. I don’t always know what caught me, exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your mind often wanders to other things when someone is talking to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it does. Like, they’ll be talking to me at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself not caring about what the other person is talking about when you miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say I don’t care. It’s more… they’re talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I still miss the middle somehow. Then I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following conversations if people spoke more slowly?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’m not sure it’s just the speed, though. Even when someone’s talking right to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them and then realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble understanding what people are saying when you're speaking in your native language versus when someone is speaking in a different language or using complex vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think it’s really about the language or big words. I mean, if it’s too technical, sure, anyone might get lost, but that’s not usually what I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting bored when people talk to you, which might cause you to miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s boredom, really. Like at the museum, someone can be telling me about the next art walk and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're interested in what people are talking about when you're having trouble following the conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I am interested. Like with the museum stuff, the next art walk and all that, I do want to hear it. I’ll be looking right at them, and then I realize I missed the middle somehow, so I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your brain is having trouble processing the information being said, or is it more that you're just not focusing on the conversation?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels more like I’m not staying with it, even though I’m trying to. Someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow the conversation better if you could see the words written down or had a chance to repeat them back to yourself?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I’m not really sure. I just know when they’re talking to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them, and then I’ve missed part of it. If I could see it written down, I guess I might not have to ask them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations in all languages you speak, or is it mostly in one language?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it in those conversations at the museum. Like when someone’s telling me about the next art walk, I’m looking right at them, and then I realize I missed part of it and have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would understand better if someone wrote down what they were trying to tell you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe if it was written down, I might follow it better. Like at the museum, when they’re talking right to me about the next art walk, I’m looking at them, but then I miss the middle and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to understand and follow conversations better if you had more time to think about what the person just said?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. If I had a moment, I might catch up better. Then I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following conversations if people spoke more slowly?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I’m not sure it’s just the speed, though. Even when someone’s talking right to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them and then realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any trouble understanding what people are saying when you're speaking in your native language versus when someone is speaking in a different language or using complex vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think it’s really about the language or big words. I mean, if it’s too technical, sure, anyone might get lost, but that’s not usually what I notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting bored when people talk to you, which might cause you to miss parts of the conversation?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s boredom, really. Like at the museum, someone can be telling me about the next art walk and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're interested in what people are talking about when you're having trouble following the conversation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I am interested. Like with the museum stuff, the next art walk and all that, I do want to hear it. I’ll be looking right at them, and then I realize I missed the middle somehow, so I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your brain is having trouble processing the information being said, or is it more that you're just not focusing on the conversation?\n\nPatient: I guess it feels more like I’m not staying with it, even though I’m trying to. Someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow the conversation better if you could see the words written down or had a chance to repeat them back to yourself?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I’m not really sure. I just know when they’re talking to me at the museum, I’ll be looking at them, and then I’ve missed part of it. If I could see it written down, I guess I might not have to ask them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations in all languages you speak, or is it mostly in one language?\n\nPatient: Mostly I notice it in those conversations at the museum. Like when someone’s telling me about the next art walk, I’m looking right at them, and then I realize I missed part of it and have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would understand better if someone wrote down what they were trying to tell you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe if it was written down, I might follow it better. Like at the museum, when they’re talking right to me about the next art walk, I’m looking at them, but then I miss the middle and have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to understand and follow conversations better if you had more time to think about what the person just said?\n\nPatient: Maybe, yeah. If I had a moment, I might catch up better. It’s just… when someone at the museum is telling me about the next art walk, I’m looking right at them, and then I realize I missed the middle and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow and understand conversations better if someone spoke in shorter sentences?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I mean, shorter might help some. It’s more that when someone’s talking right to me—like about the next art walk—I’m looking at them, and then I realize I missed a chunk of what they said. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow conversations better if someone explained things to you in a very simple way?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, yeah. I don’t know if it’s that I need it explained simple exactly… it’s more that I’m looking right at them, like at the museum when they’re talking about the next art walk, and then I realize I missed the middle part. Then I have to ask them to say it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow conversations better if someone repeated back to you what you just said, to make sure you understood it?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I’m not sure that’s the main thing, though. It’s more like… someone at the museum is talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part.
